					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">4th and 6th Prime Minister of Great Britain, 1754–56 and 1757–62</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"The Duke of Newcastle" redirects here. For other holders of the title, see <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Newcastle" title="Duke of Newcastle">Duke of Newcastle</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

</p>

</td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><span style="display:none">(<span class="bday">1693-07-21</span>)</span>21 July 1693<br /><a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a>, England</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">17 November 1768<span style="display:none">(1768-11-17)</span> (aged&#160;75)<br /><a href="/wiki/Lincoln%27s_Inn_Fields" title="Lincoln&#39;s Inn Fields">Lincoln's Inn Fields</a>, England</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Resting place</th><td class="infobox-data label">All Saints Churchyard, <a href="/wiki/Laughton,_East_Sussex" title="Laughton, East Sussex">Laughton, East Sussex</a>, England</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Whigs_(British_political_party)" title="Whigs (British political party)">Whig</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouse</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1151524712">.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin2px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-2px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin3px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-3px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-display-ws{display:inline;white-space:nowrap}</style>
<div class="marriage-display-ws"><div style="display:inline-block;line-height:normal;margin-top:1px;white-space:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Harriet_Pelham-Holles,_Duchess_of_Newcastle" title="Harriet Pelham-Holles, Duchess of Newcastle">Lady Harriet Godolphin</a></div>

href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Pelham,_1st_Baron_Pelham" title="Thomas Pelham, 1st Baron Pelham">Thomas Pelham, 1st Baron Pelham</a></li><li>Lady Grace Holles</li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Alma_mater" title="Alma mater">Alma mater</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Clare_College,_Cambridge" title="Clare College, Cambridge">Clare College, Cambridge</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Signature</th><td class="infobox-data"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle_Signature.svg" class="mw-file-description" title="Thomas Pelham-Holles, 1st Duke of Newcastle&#39;s signature"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle_Signature.svg/128px-Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle_Signature.svg.png" decoding="async" width="128" height="43" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle_Signature.svg/192px-Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle_Signature.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle_Signature.svg/256px-Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle_Signature.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="704" data-file-height="234" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Thomas Pelham-Holles, 1st Duke of Newcastle upon Tyne and 1st Duke of Newcastle-under-Lyne</b>, <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_the_Garter" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of the Garter">KG</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">PC</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a></span> (21 July 1693&#160;&#8211;&#32;17 November 1768) was an <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">English</a> <a href="/wiki/British_Whig_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="British Whig Party">Whig</a> statesman who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Great_Britain" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister of Great Britain">Prime Minister of Great Britain</a>, his official life extended throughout the Whig supremacy of the 18th century. He is commonly known as the <b>Duke of Newcastle</b>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A protégé of <a href="/wiki/Robert_Walpole" title="Robert Walpole">Robert Walpole</a>, he served under him for more than 20 years until 1742. He held power with his brother, prime minister <a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham" title="Henry Pelham">Henry Pelham</a>, until 1754. He had then served as a <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_(United_Kingdom)" title="Secretary of State (United Kingdom)">Secretary of State</a> continuously for 30 years and dominated British foreign policy.
</p><p>After Henry's death, Newcastle was prime minister for six years in two separate periods. While his first premiership was not particularly notable, Newcastle precipitated the <a href="/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War" title="Seven Years&#39; War">Seven Years' War</a>, and his weak diplomacy cost him his premiership.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> After his second term, he served briefly in <a href="/wiki/Charles_Watson-Wentworth,_2nd_Marquess_of_Rockingham" title="Charles Watson-Wentworth, 2nd Marquess of Rockingham">Lord Rockingham</a>'s ministry, before he retired from government. He was most effective as a deputy to a leader of greater ability, such as Walpole, his brother, or Pitt. Few politicians in British history matched his skills and industry in using patronage to maintain power over long stretches of time. His genius appeared as the chief party manager for the Whigs from 1715 to 1761. He used his energy and his money to select candidates, distribute patronage and win elections. He was especially influential in the counties of Sussex, Nottinghamshire, Yorkshire and Lincolnshire. His greatest triumph came in the <a href="/wiki/1754_British_general_election" title="1754 British general election">1754 election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Outside the electoral realm, his reputation has suffered. Historian <a href="/wiki/H._T._Dickinson" title="H. T. Dickinson">Harry Dickinson</a> says that he became
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>[n]otorious for his fussiness and fretfulness, his petty jealousies, his reluctance to accept responsibility for his actions, and his inability to pursue any political objective to his own satisfaction or to the nation's profit&#160;... Many modern historians have depicted him as the epitome of unredeemed mediocrity and as a veritable buffoon in office.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NewcastleHouseJohnBowles1754.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/NewcastleHouseJohnBowles1754.jpg/140px-NewcastleHouseJohnBowles1754.jpg" decoding="async" width="140" height="184" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/NewcastleHouseJohnBowles1754.jpg/210px-NewcastleHouseJohnBowles1754.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/NewcastleHouseJohnBowles1754.jpg/280px-NewcastleHouseJohnBowles1754.jpg 2x" data-file-width="382" data-file-height="502" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Newcastle_House" title="Newcastle House">Newcastle House</a> which he inherited from his uncle in 1711, and used as his primary London residence, often throwing lavish parties there.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Thomas Pelham was born in London on 21 July 1693<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> the eldest son of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Pelham,_1st_Baron_Pelham" title="Thomas Pelham, 1st Baron Pelham">Thomas Pelham, 1st Baron Pelham</a>, by his second wife, the former Lady Grace Holles, younger sister of <a href="/wiki/John_Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle-upon-Tyne" class="mw-redirect" title="John Holles, 1st Duke of Newcastle-upon-Tyne">John Holles, 1st Duke of Newcastle-upon-Tyne</a>. He studied at <a href="/wiki/Westminster_School" title="Westminster School">Westminster School</a> and was admitted as a fellow commoner at <a href="/wiki/Clare_College,_Cambridge" title="Clare College, Cambridge">Clare College, Cambridge</a>, in 1710.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Pelham's uncle died in 1711, and his father the next year, both leaving their large estates to their nephew and son. When he came of age in 1714, Lord Pelham was one of the greatest landowners in the kingdom,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> enjoying enormous patronage in the county of <a href="/wiki/Sussex" title="Sussex">Sussex</a>. One stipulation of his uncle's will was that his nephew add Holles to his name, which he faithfully did, thereafter styling himself as Thomas Pelham-Holles.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> A long-standing legal dispute over the estate with his Aunt was finally settled in 1714.
</p><p>He increasingly identified with <a href="/wiki/Whig_(British_political_faction)" class="mw-redirect" title="Whig (British political faction)">Whig</a> politics, like his father and uncle, but whereas they had been moderate in their views, he grew increasingly more partisan and militant in his views.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Britain was very divided between Whigs who favoured the succession of <a href="/wiki/George_I_of_Great_Britain" title="George I of Great Britain">George of Hanover</a> after <a href="/wiki/Anne,_Queen_of_Great_Britain" title="Anne, Queen of Great Britain">Queen Anne</a>'s death and <a href="/wiki/Tories" class="mw-redirect" title="Tories">Tories</a> who supported the return of the Jacobite <a href="/wiki/James_Francis_Edward_Stuart" title="James Francis Edward Stuart">James Stuart</a>, known later as the 'old pretender'. This issue dominated British politics during the last few years of Queen Anne's reign, leading up to her death in 1714, and had a profound impact on the future career of the young Duke of Newcastle. He joined the <a href="/wiki/Hannover_Club" title="Hannover Club">Hannover Club</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Kit_Kat_Club" class="mw-redirect" title="Kit Kat Club">Kit Kat Club</a>, both leading centres of Whig thinking and organisation. <a href="/wiki/Newcastle_House" title="Newcastle House">Newcastle House</a> in London became his premier residence.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early political career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Royal_favour">Royal favour</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Royal favour">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Robert_Walpole_prime_minister_of_Britain.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/Robert_Walpole_prime_minister_of_Britain.jpg/140px-Robert_Walpole_prime_minister_of_Britain.jpg" decoding="async" width="140" height="266" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/Robert_Walpole_prime_minister_of_Britain.jpg/210px-Robert_Walpole_prime_minister_of_Britain.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/Robert_Walpole_prime_minister_of_Britain.jpg/280px-Robert_Walpole_prime_minister_of_Britain.jpg 2x" data-file-width="345" data-file-height="655" /></a><figcaption>From 1720 Newcastle allied himself with <a href="/wiki/Robert_Walpole" title="Robert Walpole">Robert Walpole</a>. They would be political partners for the next 20 years, and Newcastle would remain a loyalist until Walpole's fall in 1742.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Newcastle vigorously sustained the Whigs at <a href="/wiki/Anne,_Queen_of_Great_Britain" title="Anne, Queen of Great Britain">Queen Anne's</a> death and had much influence in making the Londoners accept <a href="/wiki/George_I_of_Great_Britain" title="George I of Great Britain">King George I</a>, even organising so-called 'Newcastle mobs' to fight with rival <a href="/wiki/Jacobitism" title="Jacobitism">Jacobites</a> in the street.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His services were too great to be neglected by the new Hanoverian king, and in 1714, he was created <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Clare" title="Earl of Clare">Earl of Clare</a>, and in 1715 <a href="/wiki/Marquess_of_Clare" class="mw-redirect" title="Marquess of Clare">Marquess of Clare</a> and Duke of Newcastle-upon-Tyne, two titles previously held by his late uncle John Holles.<sup id="cite_ref-browningondb_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-browningondb-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> He also became <a href="/wiki/Lord-Lieutenant" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord-Lieutenant">Lord-Lieutenant</a> of the counties of <a href="/wiki/Middlesex" title="Middlesex">Middlesex</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lord_Lieutenant_of_Nottinghamshire" title="Lord Lieutenant of Nottinghamshire">Nottingham</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Garter" title="Order of the Garter">Knight of the Garter</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> In his new position, he was in charge of suppressing Jacobitism in the counties under his control. In Middlesex, he arrested and questioned 800 people and drew up a Voluntary Defence Association to defend the county. In 1715, he became involved in a riot that ended with two men being killed, and Newcastle fleeing along rooftops.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The succession of <a href="/wiki/George_I_of_Great_Britain" title="George I of Great Britain">George I</a> was secured in late 1715 by the defeat of a Jacobite army at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Preston_(1715)" title="Battle of Preston (1715)">Battle of Preston</a> and the subsequent flight of the <a href="/wiki/Old_Pretender" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Pretender">Old Pretender</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Walpole.27s_ally"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Walpole's_ally">Walpole's ally</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Walpole&#039;s ally">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The victory of the Hanoverians over the Jacobites marked the beginning of the Whig Ascendancy which lasted for much of the 18th century. Because the Tory opposition had been tainted, in the eyes of George I, by their support of the Jacobite pretenders, he did not trust them and drew all of his ministers and officials from the Whigs. Following their victory, the Whigs split with one group forming the government for George I, and the other dissident Whigs became the effective opposition in Parliament. After a period of political manoeuvring, he was for a while associated with a Whig faction led by <a href="/wiki/James_Stanhope,_1st_Earl_Stanhope" title="James Stanhope, 1st Earl Stanhope">James Stanhope</a>, but from 1720, Newcastle began to identify strongly with the government Whigs, who had quickly come to be dominated by Sir <a href="/wiki/Robert_Walpole" title="Robert Walpole">Robert Walpole</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Walpole gladly welcomed the young Newcastle into his coterie because Walpole believed that he could easily control Newcastle and because it would strengthen Walpole's hand against the rival Whig factions.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Newcastle joined with Walpole because Newcastle, correctly, believed that Walpole was going to dominate British politics for a generation.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2012)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In 1721, Walpole began to serve as Britain's first prime minister and would hold that position for the next 21 years. He was related to Walpole's leading ally, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Townshend,_2nd_Viscount_Townshend" title="Charles Townshend, 2nd Viscount Townshend">Charles Townshend</a>, strengthening his bond with the leader of the new administration.
</p><p>On 2 April 1717, he increased his Whig connections by marrying <a href="/wiki/Henrietta_Pelham-Holles,_Duchess_of_Newcastle-upon-Tyne" class="mw-redirect" title="Henrietta Pelham-Holles, Duchess of Newcastle-upon-Tyne">Lady Henrietta Godolphin</a>, the granddaughter of the <a href="/wiki/John_Churchill,_1st_Duke_of_Marlborough" title="John Churchill, 1st Duke of Marlborough">Duke of Marlborough</a>, a national hero following his victories in the recent <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Spanish_Succession" title="War of the Spanish Succession">European war</a> who was considered a Whig icon.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Lord_Chamberlain">Lord Chamberlain</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Lord Chamberlain">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NewcastleLincoln.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/NewcastleLincoln.jpg/200px-NewcastleLincoln.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="171" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/NewcastleLincoln.jpg/300px-NewcastleLincoln.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/NewcastleLincoln.jpg/400px-NewcastleLincoln.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="682" /></a><figcaption>Newcastle (left) and <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clinton,_7th_Earl_of_Lincoln" title="Henry Clinton, 7th Earl of Lincoln">Henry Clinton, 7th Earl of Lincoln</a> as painted by <a href="/wiki/Godfrey_Kneller" title="Godfrey Kneller">Godfrey Kneller</a>, c. 1721.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1717, at 23, Newcastle first attained high political office as <a href="/wiki/Lord_Chamberlain" title="Lord Chamberlain">Lord Chamberlain</a> of the Household and was given the responsibility of overseeing theatres.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Plays were often extremely political, and Newcastle was tasked with suppressing any plays or playwrights that he believed to be too critical of the Hanoverian succession or the Whig government. Newcastle clashed repeatedly with <a href="/wiki/Sir_Richard_Steele" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Richard Steele">Sir Richard Steele</a>, a leading playwright.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> In 1719, he was one of the three main investors in <a href="/wiki/George_Frideric_Handel" title="George Frideric Handel">George Frideric Handel</a>'s new opera company, the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Academy_of_Music_(1719)" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Academy of Music (1719)">Royal Academy of Music</a>. The Duke ordered Handel in May 1719 to go to the Continent to contract singers for as long as possible.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2013)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>He held the post for seven years and performed well enough to be considered for further promotion. Despite his youth, he had demonstrated his strength in several <a href="/wiki/General_elections" class="mw-redirect" title="General elections">general elections</a> when he had been able to get as many as 20 MPs elected to seats that he controlled through his family's wealth and political patronage. He survived in the office during the turmoil in the Whigs between 1717 and 1721, and his switch of allegiance to Walpole secured his influence thereafter. Walpole had overseen a brief end to the rift between the Whig factions after the collapse of the <a href="/wiki/South_Sea_Company" title="South Sea Company">South Sea Company</a>, which had left thousands ruined. Newcastle himself had lost £4,000. Walpole was then seen as the only man to bring stability to the country and the Whigs, and he was granted unprecedented powers, effectively making him the first <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Great_Britain" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister of Great Britain">Prime Minister of Great Britain</a>.
</p><p>During his time in the office, Newcastle and his wife had become famous for throwing lavish parties that were attended by much of London society including many of his political opponents. He was also prodigiously fond of <a href="/wiki/Fox_hunting" title="Fox hunting">fox hunting</a> and often went down to <a href="/wiki/Bishopstone,_East_Sussex" title="Bishopstone, East Sussex">Bishopstone</a>, one of his Sussex properties, expressly for that purpose.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> During his time as Lord Chamberlain he oversaw a major overhaul of public buildings, many of which had fallen into very poor repair.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State">Secretary of State</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Secretary of State">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Appointment">Appointment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Appointment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1724, Newcastle was chosen by Walpole to be <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_the_Southern_Department" title="Secretary of State for the Southern Department">Secretary of State for the Southern Department</a> in place of <a href="/wiki/John_Carteret,_2nd_Earl_Granville" title="John Carteret, 2nd Earl Granville">Lord Carteret</a>, a move largely engineered by Townshend. He had been for some time considered the third most important man in the government, behind Walpole and Townshend, which was confirmed by his new position. Newcastle had for several years been growing increasingly interested in foreign affairs and had been educating himself on the subtle details of diplomacy and the <a href="/wiki/European_State_System" class="mw-redirect" title="European State System">European State System</a>. However, his first few years in the office had him defer control of British foreign policy to the other Secretary of State, Townshend, and Newcastle effectively served as his deputy. Walpole was generally happy to allow Townshend to control foreign affairs, as he agreed with him on most issues.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2012)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_French_Alliance">The French Alliance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: The French Alliance">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Anglo-French_Alliance_(1716%E2%80%931731)" title="Anglo-French Alliance (1716–1731)">Anglo-French Alliance (1716–1731)</a></div>
<p>Since the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Utrecht" class="mw-redirect" title="Treaty of Utrecht">Treaty of Utrecht</a>, which had ended the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Spanish_Succession" title="War of the Spanish Succession">last major European war</a>, Britain had been an ally of France, a strong reversal in policy, as France had previously been considered the premier enemy of Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The reasons for the alliance were complex, and many had doubted the détente could last long, but when Newcastle became Secretary of State, they had been allies for nearly a decade. By 1719, they had become part of a wider Quadruple Alliance, which was by far the most powerful force in European politics, as had been demonstrated during the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Quadruple_Alliance" title="War of the Quadruple Alliance">War of the Quadruple Alliance</a>, a largely-naval war in the Mediterranean by which the powers had defeated a Spanish attempt to reclaim lost territory in Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The alliance was unpopular, however, with many in Parliament and in the country, which continued to consider France to be Britain's natural enemy.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle-under-Lyne_by_Charles_Jervas.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle-under-Lyne_by_Charles_Jervas.jpg/170px-Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle-under-Lyne_by_Charles_Jervas.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="207" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle-under-Lyne_by_Charles_Jervas.jpg/255px-Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle-under-Lyne_by_Charles_Jervas.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle-under-Lyne_by_Charles_Jervas.jpg/340px-Thomas_Pelham-Holles%2C_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle-under-Lyne_by_Charles_Jervas.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="2929" /></a><figcaption>Newcastle in 1735 when he was <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_the_Southern_Department" title="Secretary of State for the Southern Department">Southern Secretary</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Walpole_ministry" title="Walpole ministry">Walpole ministry</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Newcastle had been joined in government by his young brother, <a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham" title="Henry Pelham">Henry Pelham</a>. The two brothers got on well but were prone to have intractable disputes. One constant source of tension between them was Newcastle's poor handling of the family fortune, which was being constantly depleted through his out-of-control spending. Pelham was also considered by many to be the abler of the two brothers, but it was the Duke who was initially more successful in politics. In spite of their differences, they remained firm political allies.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_crisis">Domestic crisis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Domestic crisis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The administration faced a crisis in 1727, when <a href="/wiki/George_I_of_Britain" class="mw-redirect" title="George I of Britain">George I</a> died unexpectedly, and his son <a href="/wiki/George_II_of_Great_Britain" title="George II of Great Britain">George II</a> succeeded to the throne.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The new king had previously had exceptionally bad relations with Walpole and Newcastle and, during one altercation between them, George's poor English had made Newcastle think that he had challenged him to a duel.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Their relationship had not improved in recent years, and many anticipated the imminent replacement of the government.
</p><p>Instead, Walpole made himself extremely useful to George II, who soon became convinced of his competence and retained him in his post. The thawing of relations was helped by the friendship between Newcastle and George's daughter <a href="/wiki/Princess_Amelia_of_Great_Britain" title="Princess Amelia of Great Britain">Amelia</a>, leading many to speculate, without substantive evidence, that they were having an affair.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> By November 1727, Walpole and Newcastle's positions were both safe once more, boosted by an election victory that saw them gain 430 seats to the opposition's 128 in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Great_Britain" title="House of Commons of Great Britain">House of Commons</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1729, a rift broke out in the government over the direction of Britain's foreign policy. Townshend was convinced that Britain's principal enemy was now <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Austria" class="mw-redirect" title="Imperial Austria">Austria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Walpole and Newcastle saw Spain as the main threat to British power because of its large navy and colonial interests. Eventually, Walpole had his way, forcing Townshend from office, and replacing him with <a href="/wiki/William_Stanhope,_1st_Earl_of_Harrington" title="William Stanhope, 1st Earl of Harrington">Lord Harrington</a>. From then on, Newcastle served as the senior Secretary of State and largely controlled British foreign policy himself. Newcastle was saddened by the demise of his relative and former patron although their partnership had become increasingly strained and the new situation offered enormous possibilities to him personally.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Peace_policy">Peace policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Peace policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Together, Newcastle and Walpole managed to drive a wedge between Spain and Austria, making an ally of the latter and directing their future efforts against Spain. Subsequently, however, it turned out that Britain's long-term major rival was neither but France, which had been considered a close ally. The increasingly-confrontational actions of the French Chief Minister, <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_Fleury" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal Fleury">Cardinal Fleury</a>, soon convinced them that they had been wrong.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> This misjudgment was later used by the <a href="/wiki/Patriot_Whigs" title="Patriot Whigs">Patriot Whigs</a> to castigate the ministry for their lack of preparation against the French threat.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:George_II_of_Great_Britain_-_1730-50.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/George_II_of_Great_Britain_-_1730-50.jpg/170px-George_II_of_Great_Britain_-_1730-50.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="214" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/George_II_of_Great_Britain_-_1730-50.jpg/255px-George_II_of_Great_Britain_-_1730-50.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/George_II_of_Great_Britain_-_1730-50.jpg/340px-George_II_of_Great_Britain_-_1730-50.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1190" data-file-height="1500" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/George_II_of_Great_Britain" title="George II of Great Britain">George II</a>, who reigned from 1727 to 1760. Despite their initial violent hatred of each other, he and Newcastle grew to have a very productive relationship, which lasted for over three decades.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In general, Newcastle shared Walpole's abhorrence of war and wished to prevent Britain from getting dragged into major wars on the continent. Notably, Britain did not become embroiled in the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Polish_Succession" title="War of the Polish Succession">War of the Polish Succession</a> and indeed tried to prevent it from breaking out. Newcastle attempted to throw both the French and Austrians off-guard by being cagey about Britain's response if war broke out, but that did not stop the conflict.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Once the war had started, George II tried to push for Britain to honour its commitment to assist Austria, but he was blocked by Walpole, who insisted that Britain should not join the war. Newcastle broadly supported the same position as the king, but he accepted the decision.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Newcastle's brother <a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham" title="Henry Pelham">Henry Pelham</a> had now attained the lucrative position of <a href="/wiki/Paymaster_General" title="Paymaster General">Paymaster General</a> and had effectively replaced Townshend as the third man of the government. The three men continued what had become dubbed as the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Norfolk_Congress&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Norfolk Congress (page does not exist)">Norfolk Congress</a> by meeting regularly at <a href="/wiki/Houghton_Hall" title="Houghton Hall">Houghton Hall</a>, Sir Robert Walpole's <a href="/wiki/Country_house" class="mw-redirect" title="Country house">country house</a> in Norfolk. The three men would hold private meetings, draw up wide-ranging policies on foreign and domestic issues and then present them to parliament for their seal of approval, which their vast majority allowed them to do. Slowly, however, Newcastle and his brother were moving out of the shadow of Walpole and being more assertive. Newcastle was particularly annoyed both by what he saw as the abandonment of Austria and by the suggestion that Walpole no longer trusted him.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1735, Newcastle had largely assumed control of colonial affairs, further increasing the amount of patronage he controlled. A devout Anglican, he was also given control over ecclesiastical matters, especially the appointment of bishops and lucrative positions in the Church of England.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Newcastle's growing independence from Walpole was helped by the support of his brother and his best friend, <a href="/wiki/Baron_Hardwicke" class="mw-redirect" title="Baron Hardwicke">Hardwicke</a>, who had become Lord Chancellor.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> During the latter half of the decade, his job was increasingly dominated by managing relations with Spain, which included trade disputes and objections to the controversial founding of the American colony of <a href="/wiki/Georgia_(colony)" class="mw-redirect" title="Georgia (colony)">Georgia</a> in 1733. The longstanding peace policy was now beginning to look extremely fragile. He also acted as a mediator in the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Polish_Succession" title="War of the Polish Succession">War of the Polish Succession</a>, helping to bring the conflict to an end in 1738.
</p>
<h3><span id="Jenkins.27_Ear_and_Spanish_America"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Jenkins'_Ear_and_Spanish_America">Jenkins' Ear and Spanish America</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Jenkins&#039; Ear and Spanish America">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/War_of_Jenkins%27_Ear" title="War of Jenkins&#39; Ear">War of Jenkins' Ear</a></div>
<p>The growing tension between Britain and Spain came to a head in 1731 during an incident known as <a href="/wiki/Robert_Jenkins_(master_mariner)" title="Robert Jenkins (master mariner)">Jenkins' Ear</a>, when a British merchant captain was captured for illegal trading off the coast of <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a> by a Spanish privateer, and in punishment for his alleged breach of the strict laws forbidding foreign commerce with Spanish colonies, he had an ear cut off.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> The incident shocked Britain not so much because of its brutality but because many saw it as an outrage that Spain should have the temerity to harm a British subject simply for trading, which many held to be a legitimate occupation despite the legal prohibition in force.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1738, Jenkins appeared in Parliament to testify about his treatment. Other merchants sent petitions, and the powerful <a href="/wiki/South_Sea_Company" title="South Sea Company">South Sea Company</a> mobilised popular opinion. To many, the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish Empire</a> was crumbling, and its South American possessions were ripe for the picking. A vociferous group in Parliament demanded war with Spain. Walpole was adamantly opposed to such a policy and became a target for unprecedented attacks.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Newcastle too came under intense pressure but initially considered the demands for Britain to declare war with Spain a dangerous step, and in spite of his increasingly bellicose statements, he still considered the idea of an Anglo-Spanish alliance as late as 1739. He tried to negotiate a solution to the crisis with the <a href="/wiki/Convention_of_Pardo" title="Convention of Pardo">Convention of Pardo</a>, which agreed a sum of compensation to be paid to British merchants,<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> but British public opinion had shifted, and Walpole felt that there was no option but to declare war in December 1739.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Henry_Pelham,_Parliamentary_Art_Collection.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Henry_Pelham%2C_Parliamentary_Art_Collection.jpg/170px-Henry_Pelham%2C_Parliamentary_Art_Collection.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="221" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Henry_Pelham%2C_Parliamentary_Art_Collection.jpg/255px-Henry_Pelham%2C_Parliamentary_Art_Collection.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Henry_Pelham%2C_Parliamentary_Art_Collection.jpg/340px-Henry_Pelham%2C_Parliamentary_Art_Collection.jpg 2x" data-file-width="922" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Newcastle enjoyed a close but at times strained relationship with his brother <a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham" title="Henry Pelham">Henry Pelham</a>. The two men formed a formidable political partnership following the fall of Walpole, and together dominated British politics until Henry's death in 1754.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The British opened the war with a victory, <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Porto_Bello_(1739)" title="Battle of Porto Bello (1739)">capturing Porto Bello</a> in Panama. That led to an outbreak of patriotic fervour, and further increased the pressure on Walpole and Newcastle for their perceived unwilling prosecution of the war.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Newcastle tried to combat that by cultivating a reputation as the leading "patriot" of the cabinet. He took on additional military responsibilities and, for the first two years of war, served as a <i>de facto</i> Minister of War.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> One of his most notable suggestions during the period was the recruitment of large numbers of troops drawn from the <a href="/wiki/British_America" title="British America">American colonies</a>, whose growing manpower had previously gone largely untapped.
</p><p>In 1741, the main British campaign against Spain was a combined amphibious attack on the South American city of <a href="/wiki/Cartagena,_Colombia" title="Cartagena, Colombia">Cartagena</a>, which had experienced considerable delays. Command was awarded to <a href="/wiki/Admiral_Edward_Vernon" class="mw-redirect" title="Admiral Edward Vernon">Admiral Edward Vernon</a>, the victor of Porto Bello, who was given a force of 31,000 soldiers and sailors to take the city. The <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Cartagena_de_Indias" title="Battle of Cartagena de Indias">siege</a> proved to be a total disaster for the British, who lost thousands of men before being forced to withdraw. Although Newcastle had issued the orders and overseen the organisation of the expedition, much of the blame for the disaster fell on the shoulders of the ailing prime minister, Walpole.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Fall_of_Walpole">Fall of Walpole</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Fall of Walpole">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the wake of the Cartagena disaster, Britain held <a href="/wiki/1741_British_general_election" title="1741 British general election">a general election</a>. The result reduced Walpole's former dominance of the House to an unworkable majority. Within months, he had been forced out of office and succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Lord_Wilmington" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Wilmington">Lord Wilmington</a>. Though Newcastle stayed with Walpole to the end, he was later accused by many of Walpole's supporters of having undermined him.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Horace_Walpole" title="Horace Walpole">Horace Walpole</a>, his son, continued to attack Newcastle's behaviour for years to come.
</p><p>Newcastle continued in office after Walpole's fall and became more powerful on his younger brother <a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham" title="Henry Pelham">Henry Pelham</a> becoming Prime Minister in 1743.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Together, the two brothers and their supporters known as the 'Old Whigs' made a coalition with the '<a href="/w/index.php?title=New_Whigs&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="New Whigs (page does not exist)">New Whigs</a>', previous opponents of the Walpole government. However, there remained a strident opposition, led vocally by men like <a href="/wiki/Pitt_the_Elder" class="mw-redirect" title="Pitt the Elder">William Pitt</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_Montagu,_4th_Earl_of_Sandwich" title="John Montagu, 4th Earl of Sandwich">Lord Sandwich</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="War_of_the_Austrian_Succession">War of the Austrian Succession</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: War of the Austrian Succession">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Austrian_Succession" title="War of the Austrian Succession">War of the Austrian Succession</a></div>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Battle-of-Fontenoy.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Battle-of-Fontenoy.jpg/140px-Battle-of-Fontenoy.jpg" decoding="async" width="140" height="91" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Battle-of-Fontenoy.jpg/210px-Battle-of-Fontenoy.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Battle-of-Fontenoy.jpg/280px-Battle-of-Fontenoy.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="829" /></a><figcaption>Newcastle was a strong supporter of <a href="/wiki/Hasburg_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Hasburg Empire">Austria</a> and advocated aid to the country during the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Austrian_Succession" title="War of the Austrian Succession">War of the Austrian Succession</a>. After the war, he built his system for retaining peace around the alliance with Austria, only for it to collapse spectacularly in 1756.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1740, shortly after the declaration of war with Spain, a separate war had broken out simultaneously in Europe into which the War of Jenkins' Ear soon became submerged. In a dispute over the throne of Austria, both France and Prussia had invaded Austria and planned to remove <a href="/wiki/Empress_Maria_Theresa" class="mw-redirect" title="Empress Maria Theresa">Empress Maria Theresa</a> and replace her with their own claimant. Austria's longstanding alliance with Britain required the latter to declare war. It was also considered by many that a French victory would leave the French too strong in Europe. However, Britain soon found itself dragged into this wider war despite the reluctance of its government.
</p><p>Initially, Britain's involvement was limited to financial subsidies and diplomacy in support of Austria, but by 1742, it was apparent that a more substantial commitment would be needed if the alliance were not to end in defeat. In the same year, 16,000 British troops were sent to the continent. Newcastle was a staunch <a href="/wiki/Austrophile" title="Austrophile">Austrophile</a><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> and strongly supported aid to the Austrians. He had long thought that the only way Britain could defeat France was in alliance with Austria, a view sharply at odds with many other leading politicians such as Walpole and Pitt.
</p><p>Newcastle's position had briefly been threatened by <a href="/wiki/John_Carteret,_2nd_Earl_Granville" title="John Carteret, 2nd Earl Granville">Carteret</a>, a royal favourite, but by 1743, Newcastle and his brother were firmly in control of British policy until 1756. Newcastle now set about drawing up a fresh scheme to enhance British power on the continent, including an attempt to persuade the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Republic" title="Dutch Republic">Dutch Republic</a> into the anti-French alliance and mediating the dispute between Austria and Prussia, which led to the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Dresden" title="Treaty of Dresden">Treaty of Dresden</a> in 1745. He also approved plans for a <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Louisbourg_(1745)" title="Siege of Louisbourg (1745)">colonial raid against Louisbourg in 1745</a>, which was successful. Along with the defeat of a <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Invasion_of_Georgia" class="mw-redirect" title="Spanish Invasion of Georgia">Spanish Invasion of Georgia</a> in 1742, this strengthened the British position in North America.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Jacobite_Rising">Jacobite Rising</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Jacobite Rising">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Jacobite_rising_of_1745" title="Jacobite rising of 1745">Jacobite rising of 1745</a></div>
<p>In 1745, the Jacobite Rising broke out in Scotland and soon spread to <a href="/wiki/Northern_England" title="Northern England">northern England</a>. Newcastle feared an attack from the north by <a href="/wiki/Bonnie_Prince_Charlie" class="mw-redirect" title="Bonnie Prince Charlie">Bonnie Prince Charlie</a>, who had already gathered 5,000 men in <a href="/wiki/Derby" title="Derby">Derby</a>, and a French invasion of southern England. In the panic, a number of false rumours circulated around London, including news that Newcastle had fled to the Continent for fear that all was lost. He was forced to show himself to a crowd that had gathered outside <a href="/wiki/Newcastle_House" title="Newcastle House">Newcastle House</a> to prove that he was still there.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, his position was threatened since if the Jacobites were triumphant his estates would likely have been confiscated and he would have been forced into exile.
</p><p>Newcastle awoke to the threat posed by the Jacobites much faster than George II and many of his colleagues, who dismissed the rebellion as a farce. Newcastle organised a response, and by late 1745, he had rallied all of the southern militias and regular forces. The Jacobites withdrew to northern Scotland where they were defeated at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Culloden" title="Battle of Culloden">Culloden</a> in 1746.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Peace_of_Aix-la-Chapelle">Peace of Aix-la-Chapelle</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Peace of Aix-la-Chapelle">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Aix-la-Chapelle_(1748)" title="Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle (1748)">Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle (1748)</a></div>
<p>On the Continent, the British continued the war effort, but they were now under pressure from the Dutch Republic to make peace with the French. The Dutch feared that the French would soon launch a devastating onslaught and overrun their country. Newcastle considered that any peace that would be made would be extremely disadvantageous to Britain, and he tried to keep the anti-French coalition strong through constant diplomacy and offers of <a href="/wiki/Golden_Cavalry_of_St_George" title="Golden Cavalry of St George">financial subsidies</a>.
</p><p>Talks for a peace settlement were convened in the city of <a href="/wiki/Breda" title="Breda">Breda</a> in 1746. Newcastle was instrumental in securing the appointment of <a href="/wiki/Lord_Sandwich" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Sandwich">Lord Sandwich</a> as the British representative at the talks, as his views were very close to his own.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Sandwich's principal instructions were to delay the talks until a significant British victory allowed them to negotiate from a position of strength. The <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Breda" title="Congress of Breda">Congress of Breda</a> did not progress well initially because the participants were not yet fully committed to peace. The Allies continued to do badly by suffering severe defeats at <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Bergen_op_Zoom_(1747)" title="Siege of Bergen op Zoom (1747)">Bergen op Zoom</a> and <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Lauffeld" title="Battle of Lauffeld">Lauffeld</a>. Newcastle's brother, Henry, was now strongly advocating peace, but Newcastle firmly rejected that since he was still convinced a major Allied victory was imminent.
</p><p>In 1747, Newcastle was involved in organising a coup to put the <a href="/wiki/William_IV,_Prince_of_Orange" title="William IV, Prince of Orange">Prince of Orange</a> in power in the Netherlands. Orange wanted to continue the war with the French but soon had to apply to the British for a massive loan.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Newcastle, now aware of how close the Dutch were to collapsing altogether, reluctantly turned towards seeking a peaceful accommodation with France. He berated himself for his "ignorance, obstinacy and credulity" and half expected his misjudgment in putting so much faith in the Dutch to result in his dismissal,<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> but both the king and the rest of the cabinet retained their faith in him.
</p><p>To oversee the peace settlement, Newcastle switched across to the position of <a href="/wiki/Northern_Secretary_of_State" class="mw-redirect" title="Northern Secretary of State">Northern Secretary</a>. He secured Sandwich's promotion to <a href="/wiki/First_Lord_of_the_Admiralty" title="First Lord of the Admiralty">the Admiralty</a> although he had wanted Sandwich to succeed him as <a href="/wiki/Southern_Secretary" class="mw-redirect" title="Southern Secretary">Southern Secretary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> During the summer of 1748, Newcastle made his first ever trip outside Britain when he visited <a href="/wiki/Hanover" title="Hanover">Hanover</a> and was received with a rapturous reception wherever he went.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> When the talks got underway, they went far more smoothly, and in October 1748, the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Aix-la-Chapelle_(1748)" title="Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle (1748)">Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle</a> was formally concluded. Britain would give back <a href="/wiki/Louisbourg" title="Louisbourg">Louisbourg</a> to France in exchange for the return of <a href="/wiki/Madras" class="mw-redirect" title="Madras">Madras</a> and a full French withdrawal from the Low Countries. The issue of free trade for which Britain had gone to war with Spain in 1739 was not mentioned at all.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Newcastle was immediately attacked by his opponents for giving up Louisbourg, but many of them failed to realise just how weak the British position on the Continent had become.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Austria was also deeply unhappy as they felt the British had abandoned them and had not tried enough for <a href="/wiki/Silesia" title="Silesia">Silesia</a> to be returned.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, Newcastle was happy with the terms that had been gained, and observers on the continent were full of praise on how he had overturned such a disadvantageous situation.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Newcastle_System">Newcastle System</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Newcastle System">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Following the peace, Newcastle began to put into practice a policy that he had been developing for a very long time. He believed that the <a href="/wiki/Stately_quadrille" title="Stately quadrille">stately quadrille</a>, which had seen states continually shifting alliances, had been unstable and led to repeated wars. He wanted instead to use vigorous diplomacy to create a lasting peace that would be built around a strong and stable British alliance with Austria. Like many Whigs he saw maintaining the European balance of power, as essential. He described the process as "restoring the Old System", but it was popularly known as the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Newcastle_System&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Newcastle System (page does not exist)">Newcastle System</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2012)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>He came under continuous attack from Pitt and the <a href="/wiki/Patriot_Whigs" title="Patriot Whigs">Patriot Whigs</a>, who despised his European policy because of their belief that the previous war had shown that North America was increasingly the most important theatre of war. They mocked Newcastle for his perceived lack of vision and ignored the complex nature of European politics and Britain's relationship with Hanover and the fact that as early as 1740 Newcastle had been aware of the expanding power of the <a href="/wiki/British_America" title="British America">American colonies</a>.
</p><p>Newcastle remained extremely attentive to the Austrian Alliance. He spent several years trying to secure the election of <a href="/wiki/Maria_Theresa_of_Austria" class="mw-redirect" title="Maria Theresa of Austria">Maria Theresa's</a> son, the future Emperor <a href="/wiki/Joseph_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Joseph II, Holy Roman Emperor">Joseph II</a>, as <a href="/wiki/King_of_the_Romans" title="King of the Romans">King of the Romans</a>, a title of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Holy Roman Empire</a> that carried enormous prestige but little real power, only to see the scheme fail because of Austrian indifference. There were a number of warning signs that all was not well with the alliance, but Newcastle ignored most of them since he was convinced that neither Austria or Britain had any other serious potential allies to turn to. Referring to the election, Newcastle believed that if his scheme failed, "France and Prussia will dictate to all the world".<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> He managed to broker a compromise at a <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Hanover" title="Congress of Hanover">Congress of Hanover</a> to secure the election of Joseph. His triumph at the Congress was soon undermined by his failure to secure Austrian backing.
</p><p>He managed to outmanoeuvre the <a href="/wiki/John_Russell,_4th_Duke_of_Bedford" title="John Russell, 4th Duke of Bedford">Duke of Bedford</a> by engineering his resignation and the dismissal of Lord Sandwich, whom Newcastle had now begun to consider a dangerously ambitious rival. The ease with which he did so demonstrated his total control of British politics, as Bedford led a strong faction. He had, however, made a significant enemy, who would later try to undermine Newcastle.
</p><p>In 1752 he made a rare trip abroad by accompanying George II on his annual trip to <a href="/wiki/Hanover" title="Hanover">Hanover</a>. During the visit, Newcastle made an attempt to cultivate <a href="/wiki/Lord_North" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord North">Lord North</a>, a future prime minister, as an ally into his political faction.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> He was unsuccessful although both became good friends, and North later spoke out in defence of Newcastle.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister">Prime Minister</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Prime Minister">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_term:_1754-1756">First term: 1754-1756 <span class="anchor" id="First_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: First term: 1754-1756">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/First_Newcastle_ministry" title="First Newcastle ministry">First Newcastle ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Henry_Fox,_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg/220px-Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="266" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg/330px-Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg/440px-Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="2900" /></a><figcaption>Henry Fox, 1st Baron Holland, by <a href="/wiki/John_Giles_Eccardt" title="John Giles Eccardt">John Giles Eccardt</a>, after <a href="/wiki/Jean_Baptiste_van_Loo" class="mw-redirect" title="Jean Baptiste van Loo">Jean Baptiste van Loo</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1740</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Appointment_2">Appointment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Appointment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>On Henry Pelham's death on 6 March 1754, Newcastle succeeded him as prime minister. He had initially hoped to stay in his role as <a href="/wiki/Northern_Secretary" class="mw-redirect" title="Northern Secretary">Northern Secretary</a> as he much preferred foreign affairs, but he was persuaded there was no other serious candidate and accepted the seals of office from the king in March.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Newcastle's first task was to select someone <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons" title="Leader of the House of Commons">to represent the government in the Commons</a>. To great surprise, he rejected the favourites, <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham" title="William Pitt, 1st Earl of Chatham">William Pitt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Henry_Fox,_1st_Baron_Holland" title="Henry Fox, 1st Baron Holland">Henry Fox</a>, and chose <a href="/wiki/Sir_Thomas_Robinson" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Thomas Robinson">Sir Thomas Robinson</a>, who had barely even been considered a candidate by most.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Newcastle was largely instrumental in appointing men considered slightly weaker so that he could dominate them. Both Pitt and Fox bore a grudge over the perceived slight and stepped up their attacks on the ministry.
</p><p>In April and May 1754, Newcastle oversaw a general election, largely adopting the electoral strategy drawn up by his brother and winning a large majority. His own personal ability to have MPs elected on his slate reached new heights.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> He now felt emboldened enough to try to push through some financial reforms. He proposed measures to reduce the amount of interest paid to the <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_England" title="Bank of England">Bank of England</a> on the <a href="/wiki/National_Debt" class="mw-redirect" title="National Debt">National Debt</a>. His decision to do so may partly have been to deflect criticism that he was not sufficiently qualified on financial matters to control the Treasury.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> At the same time, he was still largely directing foreign policy, his main emphasis.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="America">America</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: America">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/French_and_Indian_War" title="French and Indian War">French and Indian War</a></div>
<p>The rivalry between Britain and France in North America had been growing for some time. Both coveted the <a href="/wiki/Ohio_Country" title="Ohio Country">Ohio Country</a>, which offered enormous potential for a new wealthy colony to be founded. Both nations sent military forces to occupy the territory. While the British set up the first initial post, they were <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Fort_Necessity" title="Battle of Fort Necessity">driven out by a French expedition</a> in 1754. Many wealthy Americans agitated for military action, but the preparations of the individual colonies for conflict were poor. There was more pressure in London from <a href="/wiki/Patriot_Whigs" title="Patriot Whigs">Patriot Whigs</a> who felt the time was ripe for British America to expand into the interior.
</p><p>In 1755, a <a href="/wiki/Braddock_Expedition" title="Braddock Expedition">major expedition</a> was planned against the French in America. A force of British regulars would be sent to seize Ohio, while another of <a href="/wiki/New_England" title="New England">New England</a> provincials would take control of <a href="/wiki/Nova_Scotia" title="Nova Scotia">Nova Scotia</a>. A new <a href="/wiki/Commander_in_Chief_for_North_America" class="mw-redirect" title="Commander in Chief for North America">commander in chief</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edward_Braddock" title="Edward Braddock">Edward Braddock</a>, would be appointed to oversee that to take over from the fractious efforts of the colonial assemblies. The architect of the scheme was the <a href="/wiki/Prince_William,_Duke_of_Cumberland" title="Prince William, Duke of Cumberland">Duke of Cumberland</a>, who held enormous political sway at the time. Braddock was a favourite, but Newcastle had his doubts about both Braddock and the plans. Newcastle had temporarily made an alliance with <a href="/wiki/Henry_Fox,_1st_Baron_Holland" title="Henry Fox, 1st Baron Holland">Henry Fox</a>, whom he also disliked. Fox was a strong supporter of the campaign and forced Newcastle's hand.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2012)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>A few months after arriving in America, Braddock's force was engulfed by disaster at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Monongahela" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Monongahela">Battle of Monongahela</a>. Attacked by a mixed force of French and American Natives in the wilderness, more than half were killed, including Braddock. The remainder retreated back to <a href="/wiki/Philadelphia" title="Philadelphia">Philadelphia</a>, leaving the French in full control of the interior.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> The Nova Scotia scheme had been more successful, but the <a href="/wiki/Great_Expulsion" class="mw-redirect" title="Great Expulsion">Great Expulsion</a>, which followed in its wake, created serious headaches for Newcastle.
</p><p>All had taken place without war being formally declared. With the decline in the American situation, Newcastle was forced to abandon his plans for financial reform, as the money would instead need to be spent on military forces.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Loss_of_Menorca">Loss of Menorca</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Loss of Menorca">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War" title="Seven Years&#39; War">Seven Years' War</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Elderpitt.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Elderpitt.jpg/170px-Elderpitt.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="213" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Elderpitt.jpg/255px-Elderpitt.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Elderpitt.jpg/340px-Elderpitt.jpg 2x" data-file-width="638" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham" title="William Pitt, 1st Earl of Chatham">William Pitt</a> was the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Patriot_Whigs" title="Patriot Whigs">Patriot Whigs</a> and a constant thorn in Newcastle's side. Many were surprised when the two formed <a href="/wiki/Pitt%E2%80%93Newcastle_ministry" title="Pitt–Newcastle ministry">an electoral partnership</a> in 1757.</figcaption></figure>
<p>While Newcastle had been paying attention to the American campaign, more pressing events in Europe demanded his attention.
</p><p>Austria had been growing increasingly tense because of a longstanding belief that the British would abandon it at a crucial moment.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Newcastle's worst fears were confirmed in 1756, when Austria concluded an alliance with France; the <a href="/wiki/Diplomatic_Revolution" title="Diplomatic Revolution">Diplomatic Revolution</a> suddenly threw the whole balance of power in Europe askew.
</p><p>Newcastle had hoped to prevent the outbreak of a major war in Europe by encircling France with hostile powers. He believed that would both deter them from attacking their neighbours and from sending reinforcements to North America.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> He thought that the only way war could happen now was if <a href="/wiki/Frederick_the_Great" title="Frederick the Great">Frederick the Great</a> unilaterally attacked Austria, but the clear disparity in numbers would make him a "madman" to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Newcastle hoped he had managed to avert war in Europe, but in 1756, Frederick invaded <a href="/w/index.php?title=Saxony_and_Bohemia&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Saxony and Bohemia (page does not exist)">Saxony and Bohemia</a> triggering the major European war that Newcastle had feared and failed to prevent. What had begun as a limited war in the Ohio Country between Britain and France now took on global proportions.
</p><p>Newcastle was widely blamed for Britain's poor start to the Seven Years' War, and in November 1756 he was replaced by the <a href="/wiki/William_Cavendish,_4th_Duke_of_Devonshire" title="William Cavendish, 4th Duke of Devonshire">Duke of Devonshire</a>. Some had even called for his execution after the <a href="/wiki/Loss_of_Menorca" class="mw-redirect" title="Loss of Menorca">loss of Menorca</a> (historically called "Minorca" by the British) in 1756.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Instead, the commander of the British fleet, <a href="/wiki/John_Byng" title="John Byng">John Byng</a>, was shot after a court-martial, which many considered a smokescreen to protect Newcastle.
</p><p>For his long services, he was created Duke of Newcastle-under-Lyne, with remainder to the <a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham-Clinton,_2nd_Duke_of_Newcastle-under-Lyne" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry Pelham-Clinton, 2nd Duke of Newcastle-under-Lyne">9th Earl of Lincoln</a>, who had married his niece Catherine Pelham.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911_1-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm1911-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Interlude">Interlude</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Interlude">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/William_Cavendish,_4th_Duke_of_Devonshire" title="William Cavendish, 4th Duke of Devonshire">William Cavendish, 4th Duke of Devonshire</a></div>
<p>Between November 1756 and June 1757, the Duke of Devonshire replaced Newcastle as Prime Minister although <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham" title="William Pitt, 1st Earl of Chatham">Pitt</a> is widely credited as the main influence on policy.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_term:_1757-1762">Second term: 1757-1762 <span class="anchor" id="Second_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Second term: 1757-1762">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Pitt%E2%80%93Newcastle_ministry" title="Pitt–Newcastle ministry">Pitt–Newcastle ministry</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Great_Britain_in_the_Seven_Years%27_War" title="Great Britain in the Seven Years&#39; War">Great Britain in the Seven Years' War</a></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Return">Return</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Return">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In July 1757, he again became prime minister as <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham" title="William Pitt, 1st Earl of Chatham">Pitt</a> could not gain enough support in Parliament. It is often incorrectly stated that Pitt was prime minister during the war, but Newcastle actually held the office. Their relationship grew into a fruitful partnership and provided a determined leadership that some felt had been lacking for some time.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2012)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
